Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, honestly, I'm just... I've been struggling a lot lately with this overwhelming sense that I'm just messing everything up. What made me come in is that I keep feeling like I ruined things for everybody. I'm a pipefitter apprentice right now, and I just can't shake this feeling that I'm a total fake in the program. Like, I'll make a mistake on a job—maybe I didn't measure a pipe cut exactly right, or I took too long threading something—and instead of just learning from it, it just eats at me. I feel like I'm letting my whole family down because they were so proud of me getting into the union, and I feel like I'm letting the younger apprentices down too, because I'm supposed to be setting a good example and I'm just... not. I know, logically, that other people make mistakes on the job site all the time. I see the journeymen mess up and they just fix it, patch it, and move on like it's nothing. But for me, it just lingers. It feels so heavy, like this massive guilt that I can't wash off at the end of the day, no matter how hard I scrub my hands. It's just exhausting carrying that around all the time, you know?

Oh! No, no, I'm sorry, I think I made that sound way more literal than I meant it. I don't—I don't have a thing about germs or feeling like my skin is actually dirty, not like that. 

I mean, obviously, working as a pipefitter, you get incredibly dirty. We’re dealing with pipe dope, flux, threading oil, old rust, just all this thick black sludge that gets right down deep into your cuticles and under your nails, so I *do* scrub my hands pretty hard at the end of the shift. We have this gritty orange pumice soap at the shop, and you really have to work it in to get the grease off so you don't ruin your steering wheel on the drive home. But once the grease is gone, I'm done. I don't keep going back to the sink or anything. 

What I meant was more... it's like a metaphor, I guess? Like, I'll be standing there at the wash station, scrubbing the actual physical dirt off my hands, and the water is running brown down the drain, but my mind is just replaying whatever stupid mistake I made that day. Like maybe I didn't tighten a fitting enough and it wept during a pressure test, and I'm just standing there scrubbing, wishing I could just wash away the fact that I messed up. The physical dirt washes off fine, it really does, but that heavy, sinking feeling in my chest just... stays. So, yeah, no literal obsessive washing, just... just a lot of emotional baggage that won't rinse off, no matter what I do.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, it's funny you ask that, because I've actually spent a lot of time trying to pinpoint exactly when this all started. I keep going back over it in my head, trying to figure out if there was one specific job or one specific mistake that triggered it, you know? But it really goes back about five months ago. That's when I decided to take on this sustainable plumbing certification course. 

I thought it was a great idea at the time, because, you know, everything is going green now, and I wanted to be ahead of the curve. My union hall was offering it, and I figured, 'Hey, I'm already in the apprenticeship, I might as well add this on and make myself more valuable.' But honestly, it's just been so much. It's not just the extra hours, which are a lot—I'm working my regular shifts, and then I have these evening classes and all this extra coursework about greywater systems and high-efficiency fixtures and all these new codes that are totally different from the standard stuff I'm just barely getting the hang of. 

So, yeah, about five months ago, right when I started that certification on top of my regular apprenticeship duties, that's when this whole pattern really kicked in. I think the added pressure just sort of broke the dam. Suddenly I wasn't just worrying about getting my regular pipefitting right; I was worrying about all these new sustainable systems, and if I messed up a regular job, my brain would immediately jump to, 'Well, how are you ever going to handle the advanced green tech if you can't even sweat a basic copper joint perfectly?' It just snowballed from there, and for the last five months, it's just been this constant, heavy feeling that I'm failing at both things instead of succeeding at one.

Honestly, no, not really. It's funny, actually, because you'd think if someone was this stressed out, it would just bleed into everything, right? Like, I've got friends who worry about everything—the news, the economy, whether they offended the barista, just... everything. And my social life, well, I don't really have a ton of time for one right now with the apprenticeship and the classes, but when I do hang out with my friends, I'm not obsessing over whether they secretly hate me or if I wore the wrong shirt. I just kind of exist. 

Even with money—like, apprentices don't make a ton, obviously, and gas prices are what they are, but I just budget it out and I don't lay awake at night staring at the ceiling, panicking about my bank account. It really is just this one specific lane. It's like my brain has this giant magnifying glass, and it's only focused on the pipefitting and the green tech course. If I'm not actively doing something related to the union or my classes, I'm actually pretty okay. I mean, I might be exhausted, but I'm not worried. It's just... as soon as I put on my steel-toe boots, or open that textbook on greywater systems, that's when the dread just drops like an anvil. But outside of that? No, I wouldn't say I worry about much else at all.

Like, I'll be standing there at the wash station, scrubbing the actual physical dirt off my hands, and the water is running brown down the drain, but my mind is just replaying whatever stupid mistake I made that day. Like maybe I didn't tighten a fitting enough and it wept during a pressure test, and I'm just standing there scrubbing, wishing I could just wash away the fact that I messed up. The physical dirt washes off fine, it really does, but that heavy, sinking feeling in my chest just... stays. So, yeah, no literal obsessive washing, just... just a lot of emotional baggage that won't rinse off, no matter what I do.\n\nDoctor: Did these feelings start after a specific problem or change at work?\n\nPatient: Well, it's funny you ask that, because I've actually spent a lot of time trying to pinpoint exactly when this all started. I keep going back over it in my head, trying to figure out if there was one specific job or one specific mistake that triggered it, you know? But it really goes back about five months ago. That's when I decided to take on this sustainable plumbing certification course. \n\nI thought it was a great idea at the time, because, you know, everything is going green now, and I wanted to be ahead of the curve. My union hall was offering it, and I figured, 'Hey, I'm already in the apprenticeship, I might as well add this on and make myself more valuable.' But honestly, it's just been so much. It's not just the extra hours, which are a lot—I'm working my regular shifts, and then I have these evening classes and all this extra coursework about greywater systems and high-efficiency fixtures and all these new codes that are totally different from the standard stuff I'm just barely getting the hang of. \n\nSo, yeah, about five months ago, right when I started that certification on top of my regular apprenticeship duties, that's when this whole pattern really kicked in. I think the added pressure just sort of broke the dam. Suddenly I wasn't just worrying about getting my regular pipefitting right; I was worrying about all these new sustainable systems, and if I messed up a regular job, my brain would immediately jump to, 'Well, how are you ever going to handle the advanced green tech if you can't even sweat a basic copper joint perfectly?' It just snowballed from there, and for the last five months, it's just been this constant, heavy feeling that I'm failing at both things instead of succeeding at one.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry a lot about things outside of work too?\n\nPatient: Honestly, no, not really. It's funny, actually, because you'd think if someone was this stressed out, it would just bleed into everything, right? Like, I've got friends who worry about everything—the news, the economy, whether they offended the barista, just... everything. But for me, outside of work and this certification, my brain is actually pretty quiet. \n\nLike, take my family, for instance. I still live at home, and things are pretty chill there. My mom handles her stuff, my dad handles his, and I don't really sit around stressing about their health or if the house is going to fall apart or anything like that. And my social life, well, I don't really have a ton of time for one right now with the apprenticeship and the classes, but when I do hang out with my friends, I'm not obsessing over whether they secretly hate me or if I wore the wrong shirt. I just kind of exist. \n\nEven with money—like, apprentices don't make a ton, obviously, and gas prices are what they are, but I just budget it out and I don't lay awake at night staring at the ceiling, panicking about my bank account. It really is just this one specific lane. It's like my brain has this giant magnifying glass, and it's only focused on the pipefitting and the green tech course. If I'm not actively doing something related to the union or my classes, I'm actually pretty okay. I mean, I might be exhausted, but I'm not worried. It's just... as soon as I put on my steel-toe boots, or open that textbook on greywater systems, that's when the dread just drops like an anvil. But outside of that? No, I wouldn't say I worry about much else at all.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I mean... no, not really depressed. I know I just talked a lot about that heavy, sinking feeling and the dread when I put on my boots, but it's not like a sadness, if that makes sense? It's not like I'm crying all the time or feeling hopeless about life in general. 

I actually had a buddy in high school who went through a really bad depression, and it was like he couldn't even get out of bed. He just stopped caring about everything—his car, his guitar, hanging out. He just kind of went blank. And I'm definitely not like that. I still get up every morning at 4:30 AM, make my coffee, pack my lunch—usually a turkey sandwich and an apple, nothing crazy—and I drive to the site or the shop. I still have energy to do the physical work, to lift the pipes and run the threader. I'm still engaged with the guys on the crew, joking around at break time, things like that. 

And when I get home, even though I'm tired from the physical labor and then having to study all that greywater system stuff, I don't feel... dark. I don't feel like life is pointless or anything. It's really just the anxiety about messing up the work. It's the stress of the job and the certification that weighs on me, not a general feeling of being down. Like, if you took the apprenticeship and the classes away tomorrow, I think I'd feel completely light as a feather. I'd probably just sleep for two days straight, but I'd be perfectly happy. So, no, I wouldn't say I'm depressed. Just really, really stressed out about my performance and keeping up with everything I've taken on.

Like maybe I didn't tighten a fitting enough and it wept during a pressure test, and I'm just standing there scrubbing, wishing I could just wash away the fact that I messed up. The physical dirt washes off fine, it really does, but that heavy, sinking feeling in my chest just... stays. I think the added pressure just sort of broke the dam. Suddenly I wasn't just worrying about getting my regular pipefitting right; I was worrying about all these new sustainable systems, and if I messed up a regular job, my brain would immediately jump to, 'Well, how are you ever going to handle the advanced green tech if you can't even sweat a basic copper joint perfectly?' It just snowballed from there, and for the last five months, it's just been this constant, heavy feeling that I'm failing at both things instead of succeeding at one.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry a lot about things outside of work too?\n\nPatient: Honestly, no, not really. It really is just this one specific lane. It's like my brain has this giant magnifying glass, and it's only focused on the pipefitting and the green tech course. If I'm not actively doing something related to the union or my classes, I'm actually pretty okay. I mean, I might be exhausted, but I'm not worried. It's just... as soon as I put on my steel-toe boots, or open that textbook on greywater systems, that's when the dread just drops like an anvil. But outside of that? I still get up every morning at 4:30 AM, make my coffee, pack my lunch—usually a turkey sandwich and an apple, nothing crazy—and I drive to the site or the shop. I still have energy to do the physical work, to lift the pipes and run the threader. I'm still engaged with the guys on the crew, joking around at break time, things like that. \n\nAnd when I get home, even though I'm tired from the physical labor and then having to study all that greywater system stuff, I don't feel... dark. I don't feel like life is pointless or anything. It's really just the anxiety about messing up the work. It's the stress of the job and the certification that weighs on me, not a general feeling of being down. Like, if you took the apprenticeship and the classes away tomorrow, I think I'd feel completely light as a feather. I'd probably just sleep for two days straight, but I'd be perfectly happy. So, no, I wouldn't say I'm depressed. Just really, really stressed out about my performance and keeping up with everything I've taken on.\n\n[Last Question]\nHas this stress started to interfere with how well you’re functioning at work or in class?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, that's the thing, isn't it? That's kind of why I'm here, honestly. I mean, I try to keep it all together, I really do. I show up early, I bring my tools, I try to stay focused, but... yeah, it's definitely bleeding into the work now. And that's what terrifies me. 

Like, just last week, my foreman—his name's Dave, he's this older guy who's been in the union for like thirty years, total hard-ass but a good guy—he actually pulled me aside. We were roughing in the plumbing for this new commercial build, a mid-rise office building, and he had to point out that I was taking way, way too long measuring and cutting my cast iron. And he wasn't wrong. I was just standing there, double, triple, quadruple checking the measurements because I was so paralyzed by the thought of cutting it too short and wasting material. He told me I need to pick up the pace or I'm going to throw the whole crew behind schedule. 

And it's not just the site work, either. It's the classes. The certification course requires us to submit these detailed isometric sketches for different greywater routing scenarios, right? And I used to be pretty good at the drafting part, but lately, I just... I can't finish them. I sit down at my desk at home, I get my T-square and my pencils out, and I just stare at the paper. I'll draw a line, erase it, draw it again, and then I just get so overwhelmed that I put it away. My instructor has had to email me twice now, chasing me down for these assignments that are past due. 

So, yeah, it's definitely interfering. I mean, I worked so hard to get into this apprenticeship. You have to pass an aptitude test, do a massive interview, wait on a list... it's a huge deal to get a spot. And then taking on the green tech certification was supposed to be this amazing career move. But now, with my foreman getting on my case about being slow, and my instructors hounding me for unfinished sketches... I'm just so scared I'm on the verge of totally wrecking both of them. I feel like I'm watching myself sabotage everything I've built, and I just can't seem to make my brain move fast enough or confidently enough to stop it.

It's not like I'm crying all the time or feeling hopeless about life in general. \n\nI actually had a buddy in high school who went through a really bad depression, and it was like he couldn't even get out of bed. And he wasn't wrong. I was just standing there, double, triple, quadruple checking the measurements because I was so paralyzed by the thought of cutting it too short and wasting material. I'll draw a line, erase it, draw it again, and then I just get so overwhelmed that I put it away. Well... yeah, I guess so. I mean, I know it's a stressful program. Everyone says the first year of an apprenticeship is a grind, and adding the extra certification on top of it, I mean, everyone told me I was crazy to do it. But... I look around at the other first-years, like this guy Tommy on my crew. He's taking extra classes too, not the green tech one, but some advanced welding stuff at the community college. And he's just... fine. He comes in, he cuts his pipe, he makes jokes, he eats his lunch, and he goes home. He doesn't look like he's going to throw up every time Dave walks by. 

And I remember when I was studying for the aptitude test to even get into the union, I was stressed, sure, but it was a productive stress. It made me study harder. This is different. This is like... paralyzing. It's like my brain is screaming at me that if I cut this one piece of cast iron a quarter-inch too short, I'm going to get kicked out of the union, fail my classes, and end up living in my car. And logically, I know that's not true. Dave isn't going to fire me over one bad cut, he's just going to yell at me and make me do it again. But my body feels like it's a life-or-death situation every single time I pick up the tape measure. 

So, yeah, I think it's definitely out of proportion. I mean, I've always been a bit of a perfectionist, like back when I was getting my associate's degree, I always wanted A's, but it never stopped me from actually doing the work. Now, it's like the fear of not being perfect is actually making me worse at my job. And I just... I don't see anyone else in my cohort struggling like this. They just do the work and move on. I feel like I'm stuck in mud while everyone else is just walking on dry land, you know? It's just so frustrating because I know I'm smart enough to do this, I know I have the mechanical aptitude, but my brain is just making it impossible.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n7\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.23, F32.9, Z56.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, I’m glad you came in. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly, I'm just... I've been struggling a lot lately with this overwhelming sense that I'm just messing everything up. What made me come in is that I keep feeling like I ruined things for everybody. I'm a pipefitter apprentice right now, and I just can't shake this feeling that I'm a total fake in the program. Like, I'll make a mistake on a job—maybe I didn't measure a pipe cut exactly right, or I took too long threading something—and instead of just learning from it, it just eats at me. I feel like I'm letting my whole family down because they were so proud of me getting into the union, and I feel like I'm letting the younger apprentices down too, because I'm supposed to be setting a good example and I'm just... not. I know, logically, that other people make mistakes on the job site all the time. I see the journeymen mess up and they just fix it, patch it, and move on like it's nothing. But for me, it just lingers. It feels so heavy, like this massive guilt that I can't wash off at the end of the day, no matter how hard I scrub my hands. It's just exhausting carrying that around all the time, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly washing or scrubbing your hands because you feel like they’re still not clean enough?\n\nPatient: Oh! No, no, I'm sorry, I think I made that sound way more literal than I meant it. I don't—I don't have a thing about germs or feeling like my skin is actually dirty, not like that. \n\nI mean, obviously, working as a pipefitter, you get incredibly dirty. We’re dealing with pipe dope, flux, threading oil, old rust, just all this thick black sludge that gets right down deep into your cuticles and under your nails, so I *do* scrub my hands pretty hard at the end of the shift. We have this gritty orange pumice soap at the shop, and you really have to work it in to get the grease off so you don't ruin your steering wheel on the drive home. But once the grease is gone, I'm done. I don't keep going back to the sink or anything. \n\nWhat I meant was more... it's like a metaphor, I guess? Like, I'll be standing there at the wash station, scrubbing the actual physical dirt off my hands, and the water is running brown down the drain, but my mind is just replaying whatever stupid mistake I made that day. Like maybe I didn't tighten a fitting enough and it wept during a pressure test, and I'm just standing there scrubbing, wishing I could just wash away the fact that I messed up. The physical dirt washes off fine, it really does, but that heavy, sinking feeling in my chest just... stays. So, yeah, no literal obsessive washing, just... just a lot of emotional baggage that won't rinse off, no matter what I do.\n\nDoctor: Did these feelings start after a specific problem or change at work?\n\nPatient: Well, it's funny you ask that, because I've actually spent a lot of time trying to pinpoint exactly when this all started. I keep going back over it in my head, trying to figure out if there was one specific job or one specific mistake that triggered it, you know? But it really goes back about five months ago. That's when I decided to take on this sustainable plumbing certification course. \n\nI thought it was a great idea at the time, because, you know, everything is going green now, and I wanted to be ahead of the curve. My union hall was offering it, and I figured, 'Hey, I'm already in the apprenticeship, I might as well add this on and make myself more valuable.' But honestly, it's just been so much. It's not just the extra hours, which are a lot—I'm working my regular shifts, and then I have these evening classes and all this extra coursework about greywater systems and high-efficiency fixtures and all these new codes that are totally different from the standard stuff I'm just barely getting the hang of. \n\nSo, yeah, about five months ago, right when I started that certification on top of my regular apprenticeship duties, that's when this whole pattern really kicked in. I think the added pressure just sort of broke the dam. Suddenly I wasn't just worrying about getting my regular pipefitting right; I was worrying about all these new sustainable systems, and if I messed up a regular job, my brain would immediately jump to, 'Well, how are you ever going to handle the advanced green tech if you can't even sweat a basic copper joint perfectly?' It just snowballed from there, and for the last five months, it's just been this constant, heavy feeling that I'm failing at both things instead of succeeding at one.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry a lot about things outside of work too?\n\nPatient: Honestly, no, not really. It's funny, actually, because you'd think if someone was this stressed out, it would just bleed into everything, right? Like, I've got friends who worry about everything—the news, the economy, whether they offended the barista, just... everything. But for me, outside of work and this certification, my brain is actually pretty quiet. \n\nLike, take my family, for instance. I still live at home, and things are pretty chill there. My mom handles her stuff, my dad handles his, and I don't really sit around stressing about their health or if the house is going to fall apart or anything like that. And my social life, well, I don't really have a ton of time for one right now with the apprenticeship and the classes, but when I do hang out with my friends, I'm not obsessing over whether they secretly hate me or if I wore the wrong shirt. I just kind of exist. \n\nEven with money—like, apprentices don't make a ton, obviously, and gas prices are what they are, but I just budget it out and I don't lay awake at night staring at the ceiling, panicking about my bank account. It really is just this one specific lane. It's like my brain has this giant magnifying glass, and it's only focused on the pipefitting and the green tech course. If I'm not actively doing something related to the union or my classes, I'm actually pretty okay. I mean, I might be exhausted, but I'm not worried. It's just... as soon as I put on my steel-toe boots, or open that textbook on greywater systems, that's when the dread just drops like an anvil. But outside of that? No, I wouldn't say I worry about much else at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed much of the time?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean... no, not really depressed. I know I just talked a lot about that heavy, sinking feeling and the dread when I put on my boots, but it's not like a sadness, if that makes sense? It's not like I'm crying all the time or feeling hopeless about life in general. \n\nI actually had a buddy in high school who went through a really bad depression, and it was like he couldn't even get out of bed. He just stopped caring about everything—his car, his guitar, hanging out. He just kind of went blank. And I'm definitely not like that. I still get up every morning at 4:30 AM, make my coffee, pack my lunch—usually a turkey sandwich and an apple, nothing crazy—and I drive to the site or the shop. I still have energy to do the physical work, to lift the pipes and run the threader. I'm still engaged with the guys on the crew, joking around at break time, things like that. \n\nAnd when I get home, even though I'm tired from the physical labor and then having to study all that greywater system stuff, I don't feel... dark. I don't feel like life is pointless or anything. It's really just the anxiety about messing up the work. It's the stress of the job and the certification that weighs on me, not a general feeling of being down. Like, if you took the apprenticeship and the classes away tomorrow, I think I'd feel completely light as a feather. I'd probably just sleep for two days straight, but I'd be perfectly happy. So, no, I wouldn't say I'm depressed. Just really, really stressed out about my performance and keeping up with everything I've taken on.\n\nDoctor: Has this stress started to interfere with how well you’re functioning at work or in class?\n\nPatient: Well, that's the thing, isn't it? That's kind of why I'm here, honestly. I mean, I try to keep it all together, I really do. I show up early, I bring my tools, I try to stay focused, but... yeah, it's definitely bleeding into the work now. And that's what terrifies me. \n\nLike, just last week, my foreman—his name's Dave, he's this older guy who's been in the union for like thirty years, total hard-ass but a good guy—he actually pulled me aside. We were roughing in the plumbing for this new commercial build, a mid-rise office building, and he had to point out that I was taking way, way too long measuring and cutting my cast iron. And he wasn't wrong. I was just standing there, double, triple, quadruple checking the measurements because I was so paralyzed by the thought of cutting it too short and wasting material. He told me I need to pick up the pace or I'm going to throw the whole crew behind schedule. \n\nAnd it's not just the site work, either. It's the classes. The certification course requires us to submit these detailed isometric sketches for different greywater routing scenarios, right? And I used to be pretty good at the drafting part, but lately, I just... I can't finish them. I sit down at my desk at home, I get my T-square and my pencils out, and I just stare at the paper. I'll draw a line, erase it, draw it again, and then I just get so overwhelmed that I put it away. My instructor has had to email me twice now, chasing me down for these assignments that are past due. \n\nSo, yeah, it's definitely interfering. But now, with my foreman getting on my case about being slow, and my instructors hounding me for unfinished sketches... I'm just so scared I'm on the verge of totally wrecking both of them. I feel like I'm watching myself sabotage everything I've built, and I just can't seem to make my brain move fast enough or confidently enough to stop it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say this stress feels out of proportion to what most people in your situation would feel?\n\nPatient: Well... yeah, I guess so. I mean, I know it's a stressful program. Everyone says the first year of an apprenticeship is a grind, and adding the extra certification on top of it, I mean, everyone told me I was crazy to do it. But... I look around at the other first-years, like this guy Tommy on my crew. He's taking extra classes too, not the green tech one, but some advanced welding stuff at the community college. And he's just... fine. He comes in, he cuts his pipe, he makes jokes, he eats his lunch, and he goes home. He doesn't look like he's going to throw up every time Dave walks by. \n\nAnd I remember when I was studying for the aptitude test to even get into the union, I was stressed, sure, but it was a productive stress. It made me study harder. This is different. This is like... paralyzing. It's like my brain is screaming at me that if I cut this one piece of cast iron a quarter-inch too short, I'm going to get kicked out of the union, fail my classes, and end up living in my car. And logically, I know that's not true. Dave isn't going to fire me over one bad cut, he's just going to yell at me and make me do it again. But my body feels like it's a life-or-death situation every single time I pick up the tape measure. \n\nSo, yeah, I think it's definitely out of proportion. I mean, I've always been a bit of a perfectionist, like back when I was getting my associate's degree, I always wanted A's, but it never stopped me from actually doing the work. Now, it's like the fear of not being perfect is actually making me worse at my job. And I just... I don't see anyone else in my cohort struggling like this. They just do the work and move on. And I used to be pretty good at the drafting part, but lately, I just... I can't finish them. I sit down at my desk at home, I get my T-square and my pencils out, and I just stare at the paper. I'll draw a line, erase it, draw it again, and then I just get so overwhelmed that I put it away. My instructor has had to email me twice now, chasing me down for these assignments that are past due. \n\nSo, yeah, it's definitely interfering. I mean, I worked so hard to get into this apprenticeship. You have to pass an aptitude test, do a massive interview, wait on a list... it's a huge deal to get a spot. And then taking on the green tech certification was supposed to be this amazing career move. But now, with my foreman getting on my case about being slow, and my instructors hounding me for unfinished sketches... I'm just so scared I'm on the verge of totally wrecking both of them. I feel like I'm watching myself sabotage everything I've built, and I just can't seem to make my brain move fast enough or confidently enough to stop it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say this stress feels out of proportion to what most people in your situation would feel?\n\nPatient: Well... yeah, I guess so. I mean, I know it's a stressful program. Everyone says the first year of an apprenticeship is a grind, and adding the extra certification on top of it, I mean, everyone told me I was crazy to do it. But... I look around at the other first-years, like this guy Tommy on my crew. He's taking extra classes too, not the green tech one, but some advanced welding stuff at the community college. And he's just... fine. He comes in, he cuts his pipe, he makes jokes, he eats his lunch, and he goes home. He doesn't look like he's going to throw up every time Dave walks by. \n\nAnd I remember when I was studying for the aptitude test to even get into the union, I was stressed, sure, but it was a productive stress. It made me study harder. This is different. This is like... paralyzing. It's like my brain is screaming at me that if I cut this one piece of cast iron a quarter-inch too short, I'm going to get kicked out of the union, fail my classes, and end up living in my car. And logically, I know that's not true. Dave isn't going to fire me over one bad cut, he's just going to yell at me and make me do it again. But my body feels like it's a life-or-death situation every single time I pick up the tape measure. \n\nSo, yeah, I think it's definitely out of proportion. I mean, I've always been a bit of a perfectionist, like back when I was getting my associate's degree, I always wanted A's, but it never stopped me from actually doing the work. Now, it's like the fear of not being perfect is actually making me worse at my job. And I just... I don't see anyone else in my cohort struggling like this. They just do the work and move on. I feel like I'm stuck in mud while everyone else is just walking on dry land, you know? It's just so frustrating because I know I'm smart enough to do this, I know I have the mechanical aptitude, but my brain is just making it impossible.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you thought about quitting the certification course because of how stressed it's been making you feel?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Quitting? No... I mean, I know logically that would be the easiest way to fix the stress, just drop the class and focus on the apprenticeship, but... no, I haven't actually considered quitting. It's just not in my DNA, I guess. 

My dad—he's an electrician, actually, been doing it for like twenty-five years—he always drilled it into me that you don't walk away from something just because it gets heavy. Plus, this green tech certification, it's really the future of the whole industry. With all the new water conservation codes coming down the pipeline—no pun intended—having this on my resume as a first-year apprentice is huge. It puts me so far ahead of the curve. 

And honestly, I fought so hard just to get a seat in that class. There was a waitlist of like thirty people, and I literally sat at my laptop until midnight the day registration opened, just refreshing the page over and over so I could snag one of the last spots. I already paid for the materials, too. The textbook alone was like two hundred bucks, and it's this giant, heavy binder full of local municipal codes and fluid dynamics, which I actually find super fascinating when I'm not in a total panic about it. 

If I quit now, I think I'd just feel like a massive failure. I'd probably be sitting at home after work, agonizing over the fact that I gave up and let someone else take my spot, and that would probably make me feel even worse than I do right now. The guilt would just eat me alive. So, no, quitting isn't really an option I'm entertaining. I just... I really just need to figure out how to get my brain to cooperate so I can actually finish the work I started, without driving Dave crazy on the site or completely burning myself out. I just have to push through it somehow.

What made me come in is that I keep feeling like I ruined things for everybody. I'm a pipefitter apprentice right now, and I just can't shake this feeling that I'm a total fake in the program. Like, I'll make a mistake on a job—maybe I didn't measure a pipe cut exactly right, or I took too long threading something—and instead of just learning from it, it just eats at me. I feel like I'm letting my whole family down because they were so proud of me getting into the union, and I feel like I'm letting the younger apprentices down too, because I'm supposed to be setting a good example and I'm just... not. I know, logically, that other people make mistakes on the job site all the time. I see the journeymen mess up and they just fix it, patch it, and move on like it's nothing. But for me, it just lingers. It feels so heavy, like this massive guilt that I can't wash off at the end of the day, no matter how hard I scrub my hands. It's just exhausting carrying that around all the time, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly washing or scrubbing your hands because you feel like they’re still not clean enough?\n\nPatient: Oh! No, no, I'm sorry, I think I made that sound way more literal than I meant it. I don't—I don't have a thing about germs or feeling like my skin is actually dirty, not like that. \n\nI mean, obviously, working as a pipefitter, you get incredibly dirty. We’re dealing with pipe dope, flux, threading oil, old rust, just all this thick black sludge that gets right down deep into your cuticles and under your nails, so I *do* scrub my hands pretty hard at the end of the shift. We have this gritty orange pumice soap at the shop, and you really have to work it in to get the grease off so you don't ruin your steering wheel on the drive home. But once the grease is gone, I'm done. I don't keep going back to the sink or anything. \n\nWhat I meant was more... it's like a metaphor, I guess? Like, I'll be standing there at the wash station, scrubbing the actual physical dirt off my hands, and the water is running brown down the drain, but my mind is just replaying whatever stupid mistake I made that day. Like maybe I didn't tighten a fitting enough and it wept during a pressure test, and I'm just standing there scrubbing, wishing I could just wash away the fact that I messed up. The physical dirt washes off fine, it really does, but that heavy, sinking feeling in my chest just... stays. So, yeah, no literal obsessive washing, just... just a lot of emotional baggage that won't rinse off, no matter what I do.\n\nDoctor: Did these feelings start after a specific problem or change at work?\n\nPatient: Well, it's funny you ask that, because I've actually spent a lot of time trying to pinpoint exactly when this all started. I keep going back over it in my head, trying to figure out if there was one specific job or one specific mistake that triggered it, you know? But it really goes back about five months ago. That's when I decided to take on this sustainable plumbing certification course. \n\nI thought it was a great idea at the time, because, you know, everything is going green now, and I wanted to be ahead of the curve. My union hall was offering it, and I figured, 'Hey, I'm already in the apprenticeship, I might as well add this on and make myself more valuable.' But honestly, it's just been so much. It's not just the extra hours, which are a lot—I'm working my regular shifts, and then I have these evening classes and all this extra coursework about greywater systems and high-efficiency fixtures and all these new codes that are totally different from the standard stuff I'm just barely getting the hang of. \n\nSo, yeah, about five months ago, right when I started that certification on top of my regular apprenticeship duties, that's when this whole pattern really kicked in. I think the added pressure just sort of broke the dam. Suddenly I wasn't just worrying about getting my regular pipefitting right; I was worrying about all these new sustainable systems, and if I messed up a regular job, my brain would immediately jump to, 'Well, how are you ever going to handle the advanced green tech if you can't even sweat a basic copper joint perfectly?' It just snowballed from there, and for the last five months, it's just been this constant, heavy feeling that I'm failing at both things instead of succeeding at one.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry a lot about things outside of work too?\n\nPatient: Honestly, no, not really. It's funny, actually, because you'd think if someone was this stressed out, it would just bleed into everything, right? Like, I've got friends who worry about everything—the news, the economy, whether they offended the barista, just... everything. But for me, outside of work and this certification, my brain is actually pretty quiet. \n\nLike, take my family, for instance. I still live at home, and things are pretty chill there. My mom handles her stuff, my dad handles his, and I don't really sit around stressing about their health or if the house is going to fall apart or anything like that. And my social life, well, I don't really have a ton of time for one right now with the apprenticeship and the classes, but when I do hang out with my friends, I'm not obsessing over whether they secretly hate me or if I wore the wrong shirt. I just kind of exist. \n\nEven with money—like, apprentices don't make a ton, obviously, and gas prices are what they are, but I just budget it out and I don't lay awake at night staring at the ceiling, panicking about my bank account. It really is just this one specific lane. It's like my brain has this giant magnifying glass, and it's only focused on the pipefitting and the green tech course. If I'm not actively doing something related to the union or my classes, I'm actually pretty okay. I mean, I might be exhausted, but I'm not worried. It's just... as soon as I put on my steel-toe boots, or open that textbook on greywater systems, that's when the dread just drops like an anvil. But outside of that? No, I wouldn't say I worry about much else at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed much of the time?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean... no, not really depressed. I know I just talked a lot about that heavy, sinking feeling and the dread when I put on my boots, but it's not like a sadness, if that makes sense? It's not like I'm crying all the time or feeling hopeless about life in general. \n\nI actually had a buddy in high school who went through a really bad depression, and it was like he couldn't even get out of bed. He just stopped caring about everything—his car, his guitar, hanging out. He just kind of went blank. And I'm definitely not like that. I still get up every morning at 4:30 AM, make my coffee, pack my lunch—usually a turkey sandwich and an apple, nothing crazy—and I drive to the site or the shop. I still have energy to do the physical work, to lift the pipes and run the threader. I'm still engaged with the guys on the crew, joking around at break time, things like that. \n\nAnd when I get home, even though I'm tired from the physical labor and then having to study all that greywater system stuff, I don't feel... dark. I don't feel like life is pointless or anything. It's really just the anxiety about messing up the work. It's the stress of the job and the certification that weighs on me, not a general feeling of being down. Like, if you took the apprenticeship and the classes away tomorrow, I think I'd feel completely light as a feather. I'd probably just sleep for two days straight, but I'd be perfectly happy. So, no, I wouldn't say I'm depressed. Just really, really stressed out about my performance and keeping up with everything I've taken on.\n\nDoctor: Has this stress started to interfere with how well you’re functioning at work or in class?\n\nPatient: Well, that's the thing, isn't it? That's kind of why I'm here, honestly. I mean, I try to keep it all together, I really do. I show up early, I bring my tools, I try to stay focused, but... yeah, it's definitely bleeding into the work now. And that's what terrifies me. \n\nLike, just last week, my foreman—his name's Dave, he's this older guy who's been in the union for like thirty years, total hard-ass but a good guy—he actually pulled me aside. We were roughing in the plumbing for this new commercial build, a mid-rise office building, and he had to point out that I was taking way, way too long measuring and cutting my cast iron. And he wasn't wrong. I was just standing there, double, triple, quadruple checking the measurements because I was so paralyzed by the thought of cutting it too short and wasting material. He told me I need to pick up the pace or I'm going to throw the whole crew behind schedule. \n\nAnd it's not just the site work, either. It's the classes. The certification course requires us to submit these detailed isometric sketches for different greywater routing scenarios, right? And I used to be pretty good at the drafting part, but lately, I just... I can't finish them. I sit down at my desk at home, I get my T-square and my pencils out, and I just stare at the paper. I'll draw a line, erase it, draw it again, and then I just get so overwhelmed that I put it away. My instructor has had to email me twice now, chasing me down for these assignments that are past due. \n\nSo, yeah, it's definitely interfering. I mean, I worked so hard to get into this apprenticeship. You have to pass an aptitude test, do a massive interview, wait on a list... it's a huge deal to get a spot. And then taking on the green tech certification was supposed to be this amazing career move. But now, with my foreman getting on my case about being slow, and my instructors hounding me for unfinished sketches... I'm just so scared I'm on the verge of totally wrecking both of them. I feel like I'm watching myself sabotage everything I've built, and I just can't seem to make my brain move fast enough or confidently enough to stop it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say this stress feels out of proportion to what most people in your situation would feel?\n\nPatient: Well... yeah, I guess so. I mean, I know it's a stressful program. Everyone says the first year of an apprenticeship is a grind, and adding the extra certification on top of it, I mean, everyone told me I was crazy to do it. But... I look around at the other first-years, like this guy Tommy on my crew. He's taking extra classes too, not the green tech one, but some advanced welding stuff at the community college. And he's just... fine. He comes in, he cuts his pipe, he makes jokes, he eats his lunch, and he goes home. He doesn't look like he's going to throw up every time Dave walks by. \n\nAnd I remember when I was studying for the aptitude test to even get into the union, I was stressed, sure, but it was a productive stress. It made me study harder. This is different. This is like... paralyzing. It's like my brain is screaming at me that if I cut this one piece of cast iron a quarter-inch too short, I'm going to get kicked out of the union, fail my classes, and end up living in my car. And logically, I know that's not true. Dave isn't going to fire me over one bad cut, he's just going to yell at me and make me do it again. But my body feels like it's a life-or-death situation every single time I pick up the tape measure. \n\nSo, yeah, I think it's definitely out of proportion. I mean, I've always been a bit of a perfectionist, like back when I was getting my associate's degree, I always wanted A's, but it never stopped me from actually doing the work. Now, it's like the fear of not being perfect is actually making me worse at my job. And I just... I don't see anyone else in my cohort struggling like this. They just do the work and move on. I feel like I'm stuck in mud while everyone else is just walking on dry land, you know? It's just so frustrating because I know I'm smart enough to do this, I know I have the mechanical aptitude, but my brain is just making it impossible.\n\nDoctor: Have you thought about quitting the certification course because of how stressed it's been making you feel?\n\nPatient: Quitting? No... I mean, I know logically that would be the easiest way to fix the stress, just drop the class and focus on the apprenticeship, but... no, I haven't actually considered quitting. It's just not in my DNA, I guess. \n\nMy dad—he's an electrician, actually, been doing it for like twenty-five years—he always drilled it into me that you don't walk away from something just because it gets heavy. Plus, this green tech certification, it's really the future of the whole industry. With all the new water conservation codes coming down the pipeline—no pun intended—having this on my resume as a first-year apprentice is huge. It puts me so far ahead of the curve. \n\nAnd honestly, I fought so hard just to get a seat in that class. There was a waitlist of like thirty people, and I literally sat at my laptop until midnight the day registration opened, just refreshing the page over and over so I could snag one of the last spots. I already paid for the materials, too. The textbook alone was like two hundred bucks, and it's this giant, heavy binder full of local municipal codes and fluid dynamics, which I actually find super fascinating when I'm not in a total panic about it. \n\nIf I quit now, I think I'd just feel like a massive failure. I'd probably be sitting at home after work, agonizing over the fact that I gave up and let someone else take my spot, and that would probably make me feel even worse than I do right now. The guilt would just eat me alive. So, no, quitting isn't really an option I'm entertaining. I just... I really just need to figure out how to get my brain to cooperate so I can actually finish the work I started, without driving Dave crazy on the site or completely burning myself out. I just have to push through it somehow.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. It's like my brain is screaming at me that if I cut this one piece of cast iron a quarter-inch too short, I'm going to get kicked out of the union, fail my classes, and end up living in my car. And logically, I know that's not true. Dave isn't going to fire me over one bad cut, he's just going to yell at me and make me do it again. But my body feels like it's a life-or-death situation every single time I pick up the tape measure. \n\nSo, yeah, I think it's definitely out of proportion. I mean, I've always been a bit of a perfectionist, like back when I was getting my associate's degree, I always wanted A's, but it never stopped me from actually doing the work. Now, it's like the fear of not being perfect is actually making me worse at my job. And I just... I don't see anyone else in my cohort struggling like this. They just do the work and move on. I feel like I'm stuck in mud while everyone else is just walking on dry land, you know? It's just so frustrating because I know I'm smart enough to do this, I know I have the mechanical aptitude, but my brain is just making it impossible.\n\nDoctor: Have you thought about quitting the certification course because of how stressed it's been making you feel?\n\nPatient: Quitting? No... I mean, I know logically that would be the easiest way to fix the stress, just drop the class and focus on the apprenticeship, but... no, I haven't actually considered quitting. It's just not in my DNA, I guess. \n\nMy dad—he's an electrician, actually, been doing it for like twenty-five years—he always drilled it into me that you don't walk away from something just because it gets heavy. Plus, this green tech certification, it's really the future of the whole industry. With all the new water conservation codes coming down the pipeline—no pun intended—having this on my resume as a first-year apprentice is huge. It puts me so far ahead of the curve. \n\nAnd honestly, I fought so hard just to get a seat in that class. There was a waitlist of like thirty people, and I literally sat at my laptop until midnight the day registration opened, just refreshing the page over and over so I could snag one of the last spots. I already paid for the materials, too. The textbook alone was like two hundred bucks, and it's this giant, heavy binder full of local municipal codes and fluid dynamics, which I actually find super fascinating when I'm not in a total panic about it. \n\nIf I quit now, I think I'd just feel like a massive failure. I'd probably be sitting at home after work, agonizing over the fact that I gave up and let someone else take my spot, and that would probably make me feel even worse than I do right now. The guilt would just eat me alive. So, no, quitting isn't really an option I'm entertaining. I just... I really just need to figure out how to get my brain to cooperate so I can actually finish the work I started, without driving Dave crazy on the site or completely burning myself out. I just have to push through it somehow.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 20 / plumber_pipefitter_or_steamfitter / associates\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Loss_of_Interest\nmanifestation: \"Lately I can walk past my sketchbook, skip volleyball, and ignore the volunteer group texts without feeling much of anything, even though those used to be the things that made me feel like myself.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah... yeah, actually. And it’s—it's really weird, because I’ve always been someone who has to stay busy, you know? Like, outside of work and school, I usually have all these things going on. But lately... I don't know, it’s like someone just unplugged me. 

Like, take my sketchbook, for example. Not the drafting stuff for the certification, but my actual art sketchbook. I've always loved drawing, mostly charcoal and ink, just sketching people on the bus or doing these little architectural studies of old buildings downtown. I have this beautiful leather-bound book my mom got me when I got my associate's degree, and I used to take it literally everywhere. But lately, it's just sitting on my kitchen island, gathering dust. I can walk right past my sketchbook every single day, and I don't even feel a tiny urge to open it. 

And it’s not just that. I play on this intramural volleyball team on Thursday nights at the rec center—we're called the Spike Tysons, it's a terrible pun, I know, but we thought it was hilarious when we came up with it. I'm usually the setter, and I love those girls, we always go out for tacos after the games. But the last few weeks, I’ve just been skipping volleyball completely. I tell them I have to study for the green tech class, but really I’m just sitting in my apartment staring at the wall. And the weirdest part is, I don't even feel guilty about missing it. 

Same thing with my volunteer group. We do this community garden project on the weekends, which is usually right up my alley because I help them set up the drip irrigation systems for the raised vegetable beds. It's super rewarding, and I usually love figuring out the PVC layouts for them. But my phone will just be blowing up with the volunteer group texts, like, twenty messages back and forth about who is bringing the mulch this Saturday or what time we're starting, and I just... I completely ignore them. I swipe the notifications away without even reading them. 

And it's just so strange because lately I can walk past my sketchbook, skip volleyball, and ignore the volunteer group texts without feeling much of anything at all. No FOMO, no excitement, nothing. Even though those used to be the exact things that made me feel like myself, you know? Like, they were my outlet, the things that made me a whole person and not just a stressed-out apprentice who's always worried about cutting pipe wrong. Now it's just... flat. I just don't have the capacity to care about any of it right now, and I don't really understand why

Like, I've got friends who worry about everything—the news, the economy, whether they offended the barista, just... everything. But for me, outside of work and this certification, my brain is actually pretty quiet. \n\nLike, take my family, for instance. I still live at home, and things are pretty chill there. My mom handles her stuff, my dad handles his, and I don't really sit around stressing about their health or if the house is going to fall apart or anything like that. And my social life, well, I don't really have a ton of time for one right now with the apprenticeship and the classes, but when I do hang out with my friends, I'm not obsessing over whether they secretly hate me or if I wore the wrong shirt. I just kind of exist. \n\nEven with money—like, apprentices don't make a ton, obviously, and gas prices are what they are, but I just budget it out and I don't lay awake at night staring at the ceiling, panicking about my bank account. It really is just this one specific lane. It's like my brain has this giant magnifying glass, and it's only focused on the pipefitting and the green tech course. If I'm not actively doing something related to the union or my classes, I'm actually pretty okay. I mean, I might be exhausted, but I'm not worried. It's just... as soon as I put on my steel-toe boots, or open that textbook on greywater systems, that's when the dread just drops like an anvil. But outside of that? No, I wouldn't say I worry about much else at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed much of the time?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean... no, not really depressed. I know I just talked a lot about that heavy, sinking feeling and the dread when I put on my boots, but it's not like a sadness, if that makes sense? It's not like I'm crying all the time or feeling hopeless about life in general. \n\nI actually had a buddy in high school who went through a really bad depression, and it was like he couldn't even get out of bed. He just stopped caring about everything—his car, his guitar, hanging out. He just kind of went blank. And I'm definitely not like that. I still get up every morning at 4:30 AM, make my coffee, pack my lunch—usually a turkey sandwich and an apple, nothing crazy—and I drive to the site or the shop. I still have energy to do the physical work, to lift the pipes and run the threader. I'm still engaged with the guys on the crew, joking around at break time, things like that. \n\nAnd when I get home, even though I'm tired from the physical labor and then having to study all that greywater system stuff, I don't feel... dark. I don't feel like life is pointless or anything. It's really just the anxiety about messing up the work. It's the stress of the job and the certification that weighs on me, not a general feeling of being down. Like, if you took the apprenticeship and the classes away tomorrow, I think I'd feel completely light as a feather. I'd probably just sleep for two days straight, but I'd be perfectly happy. So, no, I wouldn't say I'm depressed. Just really, really stressed out about my performance and keeping up with everything I've taken on.\n\nDoctor: Has this stress started to interfere with how well you’re functioning at work or in class?\n\nPatient: Well, that's the thing, isn't it? That's kind of why I'm here, honestly. I mean, I try to keep it all together, I really do. I show up early, I bring my tools, I try to stay focused, but... yeah, it's definitely bleeding into the work now. And that's what terrifies me. \n\nLike, just last week, my foreman—his name's Dave, he's this older guy who's been in the union for like thirty years, total hard-ass but a good guy—he actually pulled me aside. We were roughing in the plumbing for this new commercial build, a mid-rise office building, and he had to point out that I was taking way, way too long measuring and cutting my cast iron. And he wasn't wrong. I was just standing there, double, triple, quadruple checking the measurements because I was so paralyzed by the thought of cutting it too short and wasting material. He told me I need to pick up the pace or I'm going to throw the whole crew behind schedule. \n\nAnd it's not just the site work, either. It's the classes. The certification course requires us to submit these detailed isometric sketches for different greywater routing scenarios, right? And I used to be pretty good at the drafting part, but lately, I just... I can't finish them. I sit down at my desk at home, I get my T-square and my pencils out, and I just stare at the paper. I'll draw a line, erase it, draw it again, and then I just get so overwhelmed that I put it away. My instructor has had to email me twice now, chasing me down for these assignments that are past due. \n\nSo, yeah, it's definitely interfering. I mean, I worked so hard to get into this apprenticeship. You have to pass an aptitude test, do a massive interview, wait on a list... it's a huge deal to get a spot. And then taking on the green tech certification was supposed to be this amazing career move. But now, with my foreman getting on my case about being slow, and my instructors hounding me for unfinished sketches... I'm just so scared I'm on the verge of totally wrecking both of them. I feel like I'm watching myself sabotage everything I've built, and I just can't seem to make my brain move fast enough or confidently enough to stop it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say this stress feels out of proportion to what most people in your situation would feel?\n\nPatient: Well... yeah, I guess so. I mean, I know it's a stressful program. Everyone says the first year of an apprenticeship is a grind, and adding the extra certification on top of it, I mean, everyone told me I was crazy to do it. But... I look around at the other first-years, like this guy Tommy on my crew. He's taking extra classes too, not the green tech one, but some advanced welding stuff at the community college. And he's just... fine. He comes in, he cuts his pipe, he makes jokes, he eats his lunch, and he goes home. He doesn't look like he's going to throw up every time Dave walks by. \n\nAnd I remember when I was studying for the aptitude test to even get into the union, I was stressed, sure, but it was a productive stress. It made me study harder. This is different. This is like... paralyzing. It's like my brain is screaming at me that if I cut this one piece of cast iron a quarter-inch too short, I'm going to get kicked out of the union, fail my classes, and end up living in my car. And logically, I know that's not true. Dave isn't going to fire me over one bad cut, he's just going to yell at me and make me do it again. But my body feels like it's a life-or-death situation every single time I pick up the tape measure. \n\nSo, yeah, I think it's definitely out of proportion. I mean, I've always been a bit of a perfectionist, like back when I was getting my associate's degree, I always wanted A's, but it never stopped me from actually doing the work. Now, it's like the fear of not being perfect is actually making me worse at my job. And I just... I don't see anyone else in my cohort struggling like this. They just do the work and move on. I feel like I'm stuck in mud while everyone else is just walking on dry land, you know? It's just so frustrating because I know I'm smart enough to do this, I know I have the mechanical aptitude, but my brain is just making it impossible.\n\nDoctor: Have you thought about quitting the certification course because of how stressed it's been making you feel?\n\nPatient: Quitting? No... I mean, I know logically that would be the easiest way to fix the stress, just drop the class and focus on the apprenticeship, but... no, I haven't actually considered quitting. It's just not in my DNA, I guess. \n\nMy dad—he's an electrician, actually, been doing it for like twenty-five years—he always drilled it into me that you don't walk away from something just because it gets heavy. Plus, this green tech certification, it's really the future of the whole industry. With all the new water conservation codes coming down the pipeline—no pun intended—having this on my resume as a first-year apprentice is huge. It puts me so far ahead of the curve. \n\nAnd honestly, I fought so hard just to get a seat in that class. There was a waitlist of like thirty people, and I literally sat at my laptop until midnight the day registration opened, just refreshing the page over and over so I could snag one of the last spots. I already paid for the materials, too. The textbook alone was like two hundred bucks, and it's this giant, heavy binder full of local municipal codes and fluid dynamics, which I actually find super fascinating when I'm not in a total panic about it. \n\nIf I quit now, I think I'd just feel like a massive failure. I'd probably be sitting at home after work, agonizing over the fact that I gave up and let someone else take my spot, and that would probably make me feel even worse than I do right now. The guilt would just eat me alive. So, no, quitting isn't really an option I'm entertaining. I just... I really just need to figure out how to get my brain to cooperate so I can actually finish the work I started, without driving Dave crazy on the site or completely burning myself out. I just have to push through it somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, actually. And it’s—it's really weird, because I’ve always been someone who has to stay busy, you know? Like, outside of work and school, I usually have all these things going on. But lately... I don't know, it’s like someone just unplugged me. \n\nLike, take my sketchbook, for example. Not the drafting stuff for the certification, but my actual art sketchbook. I've always loved drawing, mostly charcoal and ink, just sketching people on the bus or doing these little architectural studies of old buildings downtown. I have this beautiful leather-bound book my mom got me when I got my associate's degree, and I used to take it literally everywhere. But lately, it's just sitting on my kitchen island, gathering dust. I can walk right past my sketchbook every single day, and I don't even feel a tiny urge to open it. \n\nAnd it’s not just that. I play on this intramural volleyball team on Thursday nights at the rec center—we're called the Spike Tysons, it's a terrible pun, I know, but we thought it was hilarious when we came up with it. I'm usually the setter, and I love those girls, we always go out for tacos after the games. But the last few weeks, I’ve just been skipping volleyball completely. I tell them I have to study for the green tech class, but really I’m just sitting in my apartment staring at the wall. And the weirdest part is, I don't even feel guilty about missing it. \n\nSame thing with my volunteer group. We do this community garden project on the weekends, which is usually right up my alley because I help them set up the drip irrigation systems for the raised vegetable beds. It's super rewarding, and I usually love figuring out the PVC layouts for them. But my phone will just be blowing up with the volunteer group texts, like, twenty messages back and forth about who is bringing the mulch this Saturday or what time we're starting, and I just... I completely ignore them. I swipe the notifications away without even reading them. \n\nAnd it's just so strange because lately I can walk past my sketchbook, skip volleyball, and ignore the volunteer group texts without feeling much of anything at all. No FOMO, no excitement, nothing. Even though those used to be the exact things that made me feel like myself, you know? Like, they were my outlet, the things that made me a whole person and not just a stressed-out apprentice who's always worried about cutting pipe wrong. Now it's just... flat. I just don't have the capacity to care about any of it right now, and I don't really understand why\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I mean... I haven't exactly been marking it down on a calendar or anything, so I don't know if I can sit here and say it's literally every single day without fail. I'm really not sure. But... it definitely feels like it, if that makes sense?
