It's just... as soon as I put on my steel-toe boots, or open that textbook on greywater systems, that's when the dread just drops like an anvil. But outside of that? No, I wouldn't say I worry about much else at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed much of the time?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean... no, not really depressed. I know I just talked a lot about that heavy, sinking feeling and the dread when I put on my boots, but it's not like a sadness, if that makes sense? It's not like I'm crying all the time or feeling hopeless about life in general. \n\nI actually had a buddy in high school who went through a really bad depression, and it was like he couldn't even get out of bed. He just stopped caring about everything—his car, his guitar, hanging out. He just kind of went blank. And I'm definitely not like that. I still get up every morning at 4:30 AM, make my coffee, pack my lunch—usually a turkey sandwich and an apple, nothing crazy—and I drive to the site or the shop. I still have energy to do the physical work, to lift the pipes and run the threader. I'm still engaged with the guys on the crew, joking around at break time, things like that. \n\nAnd when I get home, even though I'm tired from the physical labor and then having to study all that greywater system stuff, I don't feel... dark. I don't feel like life is pointless or anything. It's really just the anxiety about messing up the work. It's the stress of the job and the certification that weighs on me, not a general feeling of being down. Like, if you took the apprenticeship and the classes away tomorrow, I think I'd feel completely light as a feather. I'd probably just sleep for two days straight, but I'd be perfectly happy. So, no, I wouldn't say I'm depressed. Just really, really stressed out about my performance and keeping up with everything I've taken on.\n\nDoctor: Has this stress started to interfere with how well you’re functioning at work or in class?\n\nPatient: Well, that's the thing, isn't it? That's kind of why I'm here, honestly. I mean, I try to keep it all together, I really do. I show up early, I bring my tools, I try to stay focused, but... yeah, it's definitely bleeding into the work now. And that's what terrifies me. \n\nLike, just last week, my foreman—his name's Dave, he's this older guy who's been in the union for like thirty years, total hard-ass but a good guy—he actually pulled me aside. We were roughing in the plumbing for this new commercial build, a mid-rise office building, and he had to point out that I was taking way, way too long measuring and cutting my cast iron. And he wasn't wrong. I was just standing there, double, triple, quadruple checking the measurements because I was so paralyzed by the thought of cutting it too short and wasting material. He told me I need to pick up the pace or I'm going to throw the whole crew behind schedule. \n\nAnd it's not just the site work, either. It's the classes. The certification course requires us to submit these detailed isometric sketches for different greywater routing scenarios, right? And I used to be pretty good at the drafting part, but lately, I just... I can't finish them. I sit down at my desk at home, I get my T-square and my pencils out, and I just stare at the paper. I'll draw a line, erase it, draw it again, and then I just get so overwhelmed that I put it away. My instructor has had to email me twice now, chasing me down for these assignments that are past due. \n\nSo, yeah, it's definitely interfering. I mean, I worked so hard to get into this apprenticeship. You have to pass an aptitude test, do a massive interview, wait on a list... it's a huge deal to get a spot. And then taking on the green tech certification was supposed to be this amazing career move. But now, with my foreman getting on my case about being slow, and my instructors hounding me for unfinished sketches... I'm just so scared I'm on the verge of totally wrecking both of them. I feel like I'm watching myself sabotage everything I've built, and I just can't seem to make my brain move fast enough or confidently enough to stop it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say this stress feels out of proportion to what most people in your situation would feel?\n\nPatient: Well... yeah, I guess so. I mean, I know it's a stressful program. Everyone says the first year of an apprenticeship is a grind, and adding the extra certification on top of it, I mean, everyone told me I was crazy to do it. But... I look around at the other first-years, like this guy Tommy on my crew. He's taking extra classes too, not the green tech one, but some advanced welding stuff at the community college. And he's just... fine. He comes in, he cuts his pipe, he makes jokes, he eats his lunch, and he goes home. He doesn't look like he's going to throw up every time Dave walks by. \n\nAnd I remember when I was studying for the aptitude test to even get into the union, I was stressed, sure, but it was a productive stress. It made me study harder. This is different. This is like... paralyzing. It's like my brain is screaming at me that if I cut this one piece of cast iron a quarter-inch too short, I'm going to get kicked out of the union, fail my classes, and end up living in my car. And logically, I know that's not true. Dave isn't going to fire me over one bad cut, he's just going to yell at me and make me do it again. But my body feels like it's a life-or-death situation every single time I pick up the tape measure. \n\nSo, yeah, I think it's definitely out of proportion. I mean, I've always been a bit of a perfectionist, like back when I was getting my associate's degree, I always wanted A's, but it never stopped me from actually doing the work. Now, it's like the fear of not being perfect is actually making me worse at my job. And I just... I don't see anyone else in my cohort struggling like this. They just do the work and move on. I feel like I'm stuck in mud while everyone else is just walking on dry land, you know? It's just so frustrating because I know I'm smart enough to do this, I know I have the mechanical aptitude, but my brain is just making it impossible.\n\nDoctor: Have you thought about quitting the certification course because of how stressed it's been making you feel?\n\nPatient: Quitting? No... I mean, I know logically that would be the easiest way to fix the stress, just drop the class and focus on the apprenticeship, but... no, I haven't actually considered quitting. It's just not in my DNA, I guess. \n\nMy dad—he's an electrician, actually, been doing it for like twenty-five years—he always drilled it into me that you don't walk away from something just because it gets heavy. Plus, this green tech certification, it's really the future of the whole industry. With all the new water conservation codes coming down the pipeline—no pun intended—having this on my resume as a first-year apprentice is huge. It puts me so far ahead of the curve. \n\nAnd honestly, I fought so hard just to get a seat in that class. There was a waitlist of like thirty people, and I literally sat at my laptop until midnight the day registration opened, just refreshing the page over and over so I could snag one of the last spots. I already paid for the materials, too. The textbook alone was like two hundred bucks, and it's this giant, heavy binder full of local municipal codes and fluid dynamics, which I actually find super fascinating when I'm not in a total panic about it. \n\nIf I quit now, I think I'd just feel like a massive failure. I'd probably be sitting at home after work, agonizing over the fact that I gave up and let someone else take my spot, and that would probably make me feel even worse than I do right now. The guilt would just eat me alive. So, no, quitting isn't really an option I'm entertaining. I just... I really just need to figure out how to get my brain to cooperate so I can actually finish the work I started, without driving Dave crazy on the site or completely burning myself out. I just have to push through it somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, actually. And it’s—it's really weird, because I’ve always been someone who has to stay busy, you know? Like, outside of work and school, I usually have all these things going on. But lately... I don't know, it’s like someone just unplugged me. \n\nLike, take my sketchbook, for example. Not the drafting stuff for the certification, but my actual art sketchbook. I've always loved drawing, mostly charcoal and ink, just sketching people on the bus or doing these little architectural studies of old buildings downtown. I have this beautiful leather-bound book my mom got me when I got my associate's degree, and I used to take it literally everywhere. But lately, it's just sitting on my kitchen island, gathering dust. I can walk right past my sketchbook every single day, and I don't even feel a tiny urge to open it. \n\nAnd it’s not just that. I play on this intramural volleyball team on Thursday nights at the rec center—we're called the Spike Tysons, it's a terrible pun, I know, but we thought it was hilarious when we came up with it. I'm usually the setter, and I love those girls, we always go out for tacos after the games. But the last few weeks, I’ve just been skipping volleyball completely. I tell them I have to study for the green tech class, but really I’m just sitting in my apartment staring at the wall. And the weirdest part is, I don't even feel guilty about missing it. \n\nSame thing with my volunteer group. We do this community garden project on the weekends, which is usually right up my alley because I help them set up the drip irrigation systems for the raised vegetable beds. It's super rewarding, and I usually love figuring out the PVC layouts for them. But my phone will just be blowing up with the volunteer group texts, like, twenty messages back and forth about who is bringing the mulch this Saturday or what time we're starting, and I just... I completely ignore them. I swipe the notifications away without even reading them. \n\nAnd it's just so strange because lately I can walk past my sketchbook, skip volleyball, and ignore the volunteer group texts without feeling much of anything at all. No FOMO, no excitement, nothing. Even though those used to be the exact things that made me feel like myself, you know? Like, they were my outlet, the things that made me a whole person and not just a stressed-out apprentice who's always worried about cutting pipe wrong. Now it's just... flat. I just don't have the capacity to care about any of it right now, and I don't really understand why\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling this loss of interest nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I mean... I haven't exactly been marking it down on a calendar or anything, so I don't know if I can sit here and say it's literally every single day without fail. I'm really not sure. But... it definitely feels like it, if that makes sense? \n\nLike, the days just kind of blur together into this one long, flat line lately. I'll wake up—my alarm goes off at 4:30 AM because I have to beat the traffic to get to the job site before Dave starts his morning rant about punctuality—and I'll make my instant coffee, and I'll walk right past that leather sketchbook on the kitchen island. Monday, Tuesday, Wednesday... it's all exactly the same. I just look at it and feel absolutely nothing. \n\nAnd then I come home, and my shoulders are just aching from wrestling with pipe wrenches and cast iron all day, and normally that's when I'd want to do something to unwind, right? Like go to the community garden or at least text the Spike Tysons group chat to see what everyone's up to. But I just sit down on my couch. Sometimes I don't even take my steel-toe boots off for like an hour. I just sit there staring at the coffee table, knowing I'm tracking job-site dirt all over my rug, and I just don't have the desire to care enough to reach down and untie my laces. \n\nSo, I don't know if there was maybe one day in the last couple of weeks where I felt a tiny spark of wanting to do something—maybe there was,\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Sometimes I don't even take my steel-toe boots off for like an hour. I just sit there staring at the coffee table, knowing I'm tracking job-site dirt all over my rug, and I just don't have the desire to care enough to reach down and untie my laces. \n\nSo, I don't know if there was maybe one day in the last couple of weeks where I felt a tiny spark of wanting to do something—maybe there was,\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been feeling sad or down most of the day nearly every day?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 20 / plumber_pipefitter_or_steamfitter / associates\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Sad? I don't... I don't think sad is the right word. It's not like I'm sitting around crying or feeling miserable all day. It's really more of that flat feeling I was talking about. Like, I'm just empty. 

I mean, there are definitely moments where I feel frustrated, like when Dave is yelling at me about the slope on a drain pipe and I know I measured it right, or when I'm staring at my green tech textbook and the words are just swimming around on the page because I'm so exhausted. In those moments, I might feel overwhelmed, maybe a little down about how hard everything is right now. But it's not a deep, persistent sadness, you know? 

It's more like... I'm just existing. I get up, I go to work, I come home, I sit on the couch in my boots, and I go to sleep. Wash, rinse, repeat. I'm not walking around with this heavy cloud of sorrow hanging over my head. I'm just... I'm just not feeling much of anything at all. It's like the volume on my emotions has been turned all the way down to zero. So, no, I wouldn't say I feel sad most of the day. Just... blank. Which is almost worse, in a way, because at least if I were sad, I'd know I was still feeling something. But this? This is just... nothing.

No, I wouldn't say I worry about much else at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed much of the time?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean... no, not really depressed. I know I just talked a lot about that heavy, sinking feeling and the dread when I put on my boots, but it's not like a sadness, if that makes sense? It's not like I'm crying all the time or feeling hopeless about life in general. \n\nI actually had a buddy in high school who went through a really bad depression, and it was like he couldn't even get out of bed. He just stopped caring about everything—his car, his guitar, hanging out. He just kind of went blank. And I'm definitely not like that. I still get up every morning at 4:30 AM, make my coffee, pack my lunch—usually a turkey sandwich and an apple, nothing crazy—and I drive to the site or the shop. I still have energy to do the physical work, to lift the pipes and run the threader. I'm still engaged with the guys on the crew, joking around at break time, things like that. \n\nAnd when I get home, even though I'm tired from the physical labor and then having to study all that greywater system stuff, I don't feel... dark. I don't feel like life is pointless or anything. It's really just the anxiety about messing up the work. It's the stress of the job and the certification that weighs on me, not a general feeling of being down. Like, if you took the apprenticeship and the classes away tomorrow, I think I'd feel completely light as a feather. I'd probably just sleep for two days straight, but I'd be perfectly happy. So, no, I wouldn't say I'm depressed. Just really, really stressed out about my performance and keeping up with everything I've taken on.\n\nDoctor: Has this stress started to interfere with how well you’re functioning at work or in class?\n\nPatient: Well, that's the thing, isn't it? That's kind of why I'm here, honestly. I mean, I try to keep it all together, I really do. I show up early, I bring my tools, I try to stay focused, but... yeah, it's definitely bleeding into the work now. And that's what terrifies me. \n\nLike, just last week, my foreman—his name's Dave, he's this older guy who's been in the union for like thirty years, total hard-ass but a good guy—he actually pulled me aside. We were roughing in the plumbing for this new commercial build, a mid-rise office building, and he had to point out that I was taking way, way too long measuring and cutting my cast iron. And he wasn't wrong. I was just standing there, double, triple, quadruple checking the measurements because I was so paralyzed by the thought of cutting it too short and wasting material. He told me I need to pick up the pace or I'm going to throw the whole crew behind schedule. \n\nAnd it's not just the site work, either. It's the classes. The certification course requires us to submit these detailed isometric sketches for different greywater routing scenarios, right? And I used to be pretty good at the drafting part, but lately, I just... I can't finish them. I sit down at my desk at home, I get my T-square and my pencils out, and I just stare at the paper. I'll draw a line, erase it, draw it again, and then I just get so overwhelmed that I put it away. My instructor has had to email me twice now, chasing me down for these assignments that are past due. \n\nSo, yeah, it's definitely interfering. I mean, I worked so hard to get into this apprenticeship. You have to pass an aptitude test, do a massive interview, wait on a list... it's a huge deal to get a spot. And then taking on the green tech certification was supposed to be this amazing career move. But now, with my foreman getting on my case about being slow, and my instructors hounding me for unfinished sketches... I'm just so scared I'm on the verge of totally wrecking both of them. I feel like I'm watching myself sabotage everything I've built, and I just can't seem to make my brain move fast enough or confidently enough to stop it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say this stress feels out of proportion to what most people in your situation would feel?\n\nPatient: Well... yeah, I guess so. I mean, I know it's a stressful program. Everyone says the first year of an apprenticeship is a grind, and adding the extra certification on top of it, I mean, everyone told me I was crazy to do it. But... I look around at the other first-years, like this guy Tommy on my crew. He's taking extra classes too, not the green tech one, but some advanced welding stuff at the community college. And he's just... fine. He comes in, he cuts his pipe, he makes jokes, he eats his lunch, and he goes home. He doesn't look like he's going to throw up every time Dave walks by. \n\nAnd I remember when I was studying for the aptitude test to even get into the union, I was stressed, sure, but it was a productive stress. It made me study harder. This is different. This is like... paralyzing. It's like my brain is screaming at me that if I cut this one piece of cast iron a quarter-inch too short, I'm going to get kicked out of the union, fail my classes, and end up living in my car. And logically, I know that's not true. Dave isn't going to fire me over one bad cut, he's just going to yell at me and make me do it again. But my body feels like it's a life-or-death situation every single time I pick up the tape measure. \n\nSo, yeah, I think it's definitely out of proportion. I mean, I've always been a bit of a perfectionist, like back when I was getting my associate's degree, I always wanted A's, but it never stopped me from actually doing the work. Now, it's like the fear of not being perfect is actually making me worse at my job. And I just... I don't see anyone else in my cohort struggling like this. They just do the work and move on. I feel like I'm stuck in mud while everyone else is just walking on dry land, you know? It's just so frustrating because I know I'm smart enough to do this, I know I have the mechanical aptitude, but my brain is just making it impossible.\n\nDoctor: Have you thought about quitting the certification course because of how stressed it's been making you feel?\n\nPatient: Quitting? No... I mean, I know logically that would be the easiest way to fix the stress, just drop the class and focus on the apprenticeship, but... no, I haven't actually considered quitting. It's just not in my DNA, I guess. \n\nMy dad—he's an electrician, actually, been doing it for like twenty-five years—he always drilled it into me that you don't walk away from something just because it gets heavy. Plus, this green tech certification, it's really the future of the whole industry. With all the new water conservation codes coming down the pipeline—no pun intended—having this on my resume as a first-year apprentice is huge. It puts me so far ahead of the curve. \n\nAnd honestly, I fought so hard just to get a seat in that class. There was a waitlist of like thirty people, and I literally sat at my laptop until midnight the day registration opened, just refreshing the page over and over so I could snag one of the last spots. I already paid for the materials, too. The textbook alone was like two hundred bucks, and it's this giant, heavy binder full of local municipal codes and fluid dynamics, which I actually find super fascinating when I'm not in a total panic about it. \n\nIf I quit now, I think I'd just feel like a massive failure. I'd probably be sitting at home after work, agonizing over the fact that I gave up and let someone else take my spot, and that would probably make me feel even worse than I do right now. The guilt would just eat me alive. So, no, quitting isn't really an option I'm entertaining. I just... I really just need to figure out how to get my brain to cooperate so I can actually finish the work I started, without driving Dave crazy on the site or completely burning myself out. I just have to push through it somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, actually. And it’s—it's really weird, because I’ve always been someone who has to stay busy, you know? Like, outside of work and school, I usually have all these things going on. But lately... I don't know, it’s like someone just unplugged me. \n\nLike, take my sketchbook, for example. Not the drafting stuff for the certification, but my actual art sketchbook. I've always loved drawing, mostly charcoal and ink, just sketching people on the bus or doing these little architectural studies of old buildings downtown. I have this beautiful leather-bound book my mom got me when I got my associate's degree, and I used to take it literally everywhere. But lately, it's just sitting on my kitchen island, gathering dust. I can walk right past my sketchbook every single day, and I don't even feel a tiny urge to open it. \n\nAnd it’s not just that. I play on this intramural volleyball team on Thursday nights at the rec center—we're called the Spike Tysons, it's a terrible pun, I know, but we thought it was hilarious when we came up with it. I'm usually the setter, and I love those girls, we always go out for tacos after the games. But the last few weeks, I’ve just been skipping volleyball completely. I tell them I have to study for the green tech class, but really I’m just sitting in my apartment staring at the wall. And the weirdest part is, I don't even feel guilty about missing it. \n\nSame thing with my volunteer group. We do this community garden project on the weekends, which is usually right up my alley because I help them set up the drip irrigation systems for the raised vegetable beds. It's super rewarding, and I usually love figuring out the PVC layouts for them. But my phone will just be blowing up with the volunteer group texts, like, twenty messages back and forth about who is bringing the mulch this Saturday or what time we're starting, and I just... I completely ignore them. I swipe the notifications away without even reading them. \n\nAnd it's just so strange because lately I can walk past my sketchbook, skip volleyball, and ignore the volunteer group texts without feeling much of anything at all. No FOMO, no excitement, nothing. Even though those used to be the exact things that made me feel like myself, you know? Like, they were my outlet, the things that made me a whole person and not just a stressed-out apprentice who's always worried about cutting pipe wrong. Now it's just... flat. I just don't have the capacity to care about any of it right now, and I don't really understand why\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling this loss of interest nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I mean... I haven't exactly been marking it down on a calendar or anything, so I don't know if I can sit here and say it's literally every single day without fail. I'm really not sure. But... it definitely feels like it, if that makes sense? \n\nLike, the days just kind of blur together into this one long, flat line lately. I'll wake up—my alarm goes off at 4:30 AM because I have to beat the traffic to get to the job site before Dave starts his morning rant about punctuality—and I'll make my instant coffee, and I'll walk right past that leather sketchbook on the kitchen island. Monday, Tuesday, Wednesday... it's all exactly the same. I just look at it and feel absolutely nothing. \n\nAnd then I come home, and my shoulders are just aching from wrestling with pipe wrenches and cast iron all day, and normally that's when I'd want to do something to unwind, right? Like go to the community garden or at least text the Spike Tysons group chat to see what everyone's up to. But I just sit down on my couch. Sometimes I don't even take my steel-toe boots off for like an hour. I just sit there staring at the coffee table, knowing I'm tracking job-site dirt all over my rug, and I just don't have the desire to care enough to reach down and untie my laces. \n\nSo, I don't know if there was maybe one day in the last couple of weeks where I felt a tiny spark of wanting to do something—maybe there was,\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Sad? I don't... I don't think sad is the right word. It's not like I'm sitting around crying or feeling miserable all day. It's really more of that flat feeling I was talking about. Like, I'm just empty. \n\nI mean, there are definitely moments where I feel frustrated, like when Dave is yelling at me about the slope on a drain pipe and I know I measured it right, or when I'm staring at my green tech textbook and the words are just swimming around on the page because I'm so exhausted. In those moments, I might feel overwhelmed, maybe a little down about how hard everything is right now. But it's not a deep, persistent sadness, you know? \n\nIt's more like... I'm just existing. I get up, I go to work, I come home, I sit on the couch in my boots, and I go to sleep. Wash, rinse, repeat. I'm not walking around with this heavy cloud of sorrow hanging over my head. I'm just... I'm just not feeling much of anything at all. It's like the volume on my emotions has been turned all the way down to zero. So, no, I wouldn't say I feel sad most of the day. Just... blank. Which is almost worse, in a way, because at least if I were sad, I'd know I was still feeling something. But this? This is just... nothing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. In those moments, I might feel overwhelmed, maybe a little down about how hard everything is right now. But it's not a deep, persistent sadness, you know? \n\nIt's more like... I'm just existing. I get up, I go to work, I come home, I sit on the couch in my boots, and I go to sleep. Wash, rinse, repeat. I'm not walking around with this heavy cloud of sorrow hanging over my head. I'm just... I'm just not feeling much of anything at all. It's like the volume on my emotions has been turned all the way down to zero. So, no, I wouldn't say I feel sad most of the day. Just... blank. Which is almost worse, in a way, because at least if I were sad, I'd know I was still feeling something. But this? This is just... nothing.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you had trouble sleeping lately?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 20 / plumber_pipefitter_or_steamfitter / associates\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Sleep_Disturbance\nmanifestation: \"For a while I was getting by on three or four hours and still showing up wired for the job and class, but now I either can't fall asleep or I wake before my alarm feeling like I never rested.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared.     "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Sleep? Oh man, sleep has been... it's been a whole thing, honestly. It's like my body doesn't even know what a normal sleep schedule is anymore. 

A few months ago, or maybe it was longer, I don't even know—but for a while there, I was barely sleeping at all. I'd be up until like 1:00 or 2:00 in the morning, just buzzing, you know? I'd be reorganizing my entire toolbox, color-coding my wrenches, or sketching out these massive, elaborate rainwater harvesting systems for my green tech class. And then my alarm would go off at 4:30 AM for work, and I'd just pop right up. Three, maybe four hours of sleep, tops. And the crazy thing is, I didn't even feel tired! I'd show up to the job site completely wired, practically vibrating with energy, ready to rough-in a whole commercial bathroom by myself before Dave even had his coffee. I felt like I had cracked the code to having more hours in the day. 

But now? Now it's a completely different story. It's like the pendulum swung all the way to the other side, but in the worst way possible. I'll get into bed at a decent time, you know, because I'm just so physically exhausted from hauling pipe around all day and my shoulders are screaming, but I just... I can't fall asleep. I'll lie there staring at the ceiling fan, watching it spin, and my brain is just doing this weird, slow-motion crawl. Or, if I do finally manage to drift off, I'll wake up at like 3:00 AM, way before my alarm, and I just lie there in the dark. 

And when that 4:30 alarm finally does go off, I feel like I've been hit by a truck. It's like I never even closed my eyes. I drag myself out of bed, and my body feels like it's made of lead. It's just so frustrating because I'm so tired all the time now, but when I actually try to sleep, my body just refuses to cooperate. It's either I can't fall asleep in the first place, or I wake up way too early and just lie there feeling like I haven't rested for a single second. It makes getting through the day at the site so much harder, especially when I have to stay focused on safety stuff and not, you know, accidentally flood a basement because I'm too exhausted to double-check a fitting.

We’re dealing with pipe dope, flux, threading oil, old rust, just all this thick black sludge that gets right down deep into your cuticles and under your nails, so I *do* scrub my hands pretty hard at the end of the shift. We have this gritty orange pumice soap at the shop, and you really have to work it in to get the grease off so you don't ruin your steering wheel on the drive home. But once the grease is gone, I'm done. I don't keep going back to the sink or anything. \n\nWhat I meant was more... it's like a metaphor, I guess? Like, I'll be standing there at the wash station, scrubbing the actual physical dirt off my hands, and the water is running brown down the drain, but my mind is just replaying whatever stupid mistake I made that day. Like maybe I didn't tighten a fitting enough and it wept during a pressure test, and I'm just standing there scrubbing, wishing I could just wash away the fact that I messed up. The physical dirt washes off fine, it really does, but that heavy, sinking feeling in my chest just... stays. So, yeah, no literal obsessive washing, just... just a lot of emotional baggage that won't rinse off, no matter what I do.\n\nDoctor: Did these feelings start after a specific problem or change at work?\n\nPatient: Well, it's funny you ask that, because I've actually spent a lot of time trying to pinpoint exactly when this all started. I keep going back over it in my head, trying to figure out if there was one specific job or one specific mistake that triggered it, you know? But it really goes back about five months ago. That's when I decided to take on this sustainable plumbing certification course. \n\nI thought it was a great idea at the time, because, you know, everything is going green now, and I wanted to be ahead of the curve. My union hall was offering it, and I figured, 'Hey, I'm already in the apprenticeship, I might as well add this on and make myself more valuable.' But honestly, it's just been so much. It's not just the extra hours, which are a lot—I'm working my regular shifts, and then I have these evening classes and all this extra coursework about greywater systems and high-efficiency fixtures and all these new codes that are totally different from the standard stuff I'm just barely getting the hang of. \n\nSo, yeah, about five months ago, right when I started that certification on top of my regular apprenticeship duties, that's when this whole pattern really kicked in. I think the added pressure just sort of broke the dam. Suddenly I wasn't just worrying about getting my regular pipefitting right; I was worrying about all these new sustainable systems, and if I messed up a regular job, my brain would immediately jump to, 'Well, how are you ever going to handle the advanced green tech if you can't even sweat a basic copper joint perfectly?' It just snowballed from there, and for the last five months, it's just been this constant, heavy feeling that I'm failing at both things instead of succeeding at one.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry a lot about things outside of work too?\n\nPatient: Honestly, no, not really. It's funny, actually, because you'd think if someone was this stressed out, it would just bleed into everything, right? Like, I've got friends who worry about everything—the news, the economy, whether they offended the barista, just... everything. But for me, outside of work and this certification, my brain is actually pretty quiet. \n\nLike, take my family, for instance. I still live at home, and things are pretty chill there. My mom handles her stuff, my dad handles his, and I don't really sit around stressing about their health or if the house is going to fall apart or anything like that. And my social life, well, I don't really have a ton of time for one right now with the apprenticeship and the classes, but when I do hang out with my friends, I'm not obsessing over whether they secretly hate me or if I wore the wrong shirt. I just kind of exist. \n\nEven with money—like, apprentices don't make a ton, obviously, and gas prices are what they are, but I just budget it out and I don't lay awake at night staring at the ceiling, panicking about my bank account. It really is just this one specific lane. It's like my brain has this giant magnifying glass, and it's only focused on the pipefitting and the green tech course. If I'm not actively doing something related to the union or my classes, I'm actually pretty okay. I mean, I might be exhausted, but I'm not worried. It's just... as soon as I put on my steel-toe boots, or open that textbook on greywater systems, that's when the dread just drops like an anvil. But outside of that? No, I wouldn't say I worry about much else at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed much of the time?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean... no, not really depressed. I know I just talked a lot about that heavy, sinking feeling and the dread when I put on my boots, but it's not like a sadness, if that makes sense? It's not like I'm crying all the time or feeling hopeless about life in general. \n\nI actually had a buddy in high school who went through a really bad depression, and it was like he couldn't even get out of bed. He just stopped caring about everything—his car, his guitar, hanging out. He just kind of went blank. And I'm definitely not like that. I still get up every morning at 4:30 AM, make my coffee, pack my lunch—usually a turkey sandwich and an apple, nothing crazy—and I drive to the site or the shop. I still have energy to do the physical work, to lift the pipes and run the threader. I'm still engaged with the guys on the crew, joking around at break time, things like that. \n\nAnd when I get home, even though I'm tired from the physical labor and then having to study all that greywater system stuff, I don't feel... dark. I don't feel like life is pointless or anything. It's really just the anxiety about messing up the work. It's the stress of the job and the certification that weighs on me, not a general feeling of being down. Like, if you took the apprenticeship and the classes away tomorrow, I think I'd feel completely light as a feather. I'd probably just sleep for two days straight, but I'd be perfectly happy. So, no, I wouldn't say I'm depressed. Just really, really stressed out about my performance and keeping up with everything I've taken on.\n\nDoctor: Has this stress started to interfere with how well you’re functioning at work or in class?\n\nPatient: Well, that's the thing, isn't it? That's kind of why I'm here, honestly. I mean, I try to keep it all together, I really do. I show up early, I bring my tools, I try to stay focused, but... yeah, it's definitely bleeding into the work now. And that's what terrifies me. \n\nLike, just last week, my foreman—his name's Dave, he's this older guy who's been in the union for like thirty years, total hard-ass but a good guy—he actually pulled me aside. We were roughing in the plumbing for this new commercial build, a mid-rise office building, and he had to point out that I was taking way, way too long measuring and cutting my cast iron. And he wasn't wrong. I was just standing there, double, triple, quadruple checking the measurements because I was so paralyzed by the thought of cutting it too short and wasting material. He told me I need to pick up the pace or I'm going to throw the whole crew behind schedule. \n\nAnd it's not just the site work, either. It's the classes. The certification course requires us to submit these detailed isometric sketches for different greywater routing scenarios, right? And I used to be pretty good at the drafting part, but lately, I just... I can't finish them. I sit down at my desk at home, I get my T-square and my pencils out, and I just stare at the paper. I'll draw a line, erase it, draw it again, and then I just get so overwhelmed that I put it away. My instructor has had to email me twice now, chasing me down for these assignments that are past due. \n\nSo, yeah, it's definitely interfering. I mean, I worked so hard to get into this apprenticeship. You have to pass an aptitude test, do a massive interview, wait on a list... it's a huge deal to get a spot. And then taking on the green tech certification was supposed to be this amazing career move. But now, with my foreman getting on my case about being slow, and my instructors hounding me for unfinished sketches... I'm just so scared I'm on the verge of totally wrecking both of them. I feel like I'm watching myself sabotage everything I've built, and I just can't seem to make my brain move fast enough or confidently enough to stop it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say this stress feels out of proportion to what most people in your situation would feel?\n\nPatient: Well... yeah, I guess so. I mean, I know it's a stressful program. Everyone says the first year of an apprenticeship is a grind, and adding the extra certification on top of it, I mean, everyone told me I was crazy to do it. But... I look around at the other first-years, like this guy Tommy on my crew. He's taking extra classes too, not the green tech one, but some advanced welding stuff at the community college. And he's just... fine. He comes in, he cuts his pipe, he makes jokes, he eats his lunch, and he goes home. He doesn't look like he's going to throw up every time Dave walks by. \n\nAnd I remember when I was studying for the aptitude test to even get into the union, I was stressed, sure, but it was a productive stress. It made me study harder. This is different. This is like... paralyzing. It's like my brain is screaming at me that if I cut this one piece of cast iron a quarter-inch too short, I'm going to get kicked out of the union, fail my classes, and end up living in my car. And logically, I know that's not true. Dave isn't going to fire me over one bad cut, he's just going to yell at me and make me do it again. But my body feels like it's a life-or-death situation every single time I pick up the tape measure. \n\nSo, yeah, I think it's definitely out of proportion. I mean, I've always been a bit of a perfectionist, like back when I was getting my associate's degree, I always wanted A's, but it never stopped me from actually doing the work. Now, it's like the fear of not being perfect is actually making me worse at my job. And I just... I don't see anyone else in my cohort struggling like this. They just do the work and move on. I feel like I'm stuck in mud while everyone else is just walking on dry land, you know? It's just so frustrating because I know I'm smart enough to do this, I know I have the mechanical aptitude, but my brain is just making it impossible.\n\nDoctor: Have you thought about quitting the certification course because of how stressed it's been making you feel?\n\nPatient: Quitting? No... I mean, I know logically that would be the easiest way to fix the stress, just drop the class and focus on the apprenticeship, but... no, I haven't actually considered quitting. It's just not in my DNA, I guess. \n\nMy dad—he's an electrician, actually, been doing it for like twenty-five years—he always drilled it into me that you don't walk away from something just because it gets heavy. Plus, this green tech certification, it's really the future of the whole industry. With all the new water conservation codes coming down the pipeline—no pun intended—having this on my resume as a first-year apprentice is huge. It puts me so far ahead of the curve. \n\nAnd honestly, I fought so hard just to get a seat in that class. I'd probably be sitting at home after work, agonizing over the fact that I gave up and let someone else take my spot, and that would probably make me feel even worse than I do right now. The guilt would just eat me alive. So, no, quitting isn't really an option I'm entertaining. I just... I really just need to figure out how to get my brain to cooperate so I can actually finish the work I started, without driving Dave crazy on the site or completely burning myself out. I just have to push through it somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, actually. Talk? Oh my gosh, yes. I mean, it's actually kind of embarrassing to think about now, but at the time, I didn't even realize I was doing it. It was like my brain was moving so fast, and my mouth was just desperately trying to keep up with all the thoughts.

And I just started talking. And talking. And I couldn't stop. I was explaining the whole thing, from the initial pipe routing to the filtration setup, and I just kept going. I was talking over the instructor, talking over the other students. It was like I physically couldn't hit the brakes. I'd be barely taking a breath, just pushing the words out as fast as I could because if I stopped, I felt like I'd lose the thread of this incredibly important, brilliant idea I had.

And it wasn't just in class, either. It was at the shop, too. I'd be trying to explain to Dave or Marco or whoever was around why we should organize the entire supply room differently, or how I figured out a faster way to sweat a joint. And I could see them, you know? I could see them looking at their watches, or shifting their weight, or literally picking up their tools to try and get back to work, but I just kept talking right at them. It was like I had this overwhelming urge to just keep speaking, and it didn't matter if they were listening or not. I just had to get it all out. It was exhausting for them, I'm sure, but for me, it just felt like my words were spilling out faster than I could control them.

Like, I'll make a mistake on a job—maybe I didn't measure a pipe cut exactly right, or I took too long threading something—and instead of just learning from it, it just eats at me. I feel like I'm letting my whole family down because they were so proud of me getting into the union, and I feel like I'm letting the younger apprentices down too, because I'm supposed to be setting a good example and I'm just... not. I know, logically, that other people make mistakes on the job site all the time. I see the journeymen mess up and they just fix it, patch it, and move on like it's nothing. But for me, it just lingers. It feels so heavy, like this massive guilt that I can't wash off at the end of the day, no matter how hard I scrub my hands. It's just exhausting carrying that around all the time, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly washing or scrubbing your hands because you feel like they’re still not clean enough?\n\nPatient: Oh! We have this gritty orange pumice soap at the shop, and you really have to work it in to get the grease off so you don't ruin your steering wheel on the drive home. Like maybe I didn't tighten a fitting enough and it wept during a pressure test, and I'm just standing there scrubbing, wishing I could just wash away the fact that I messed up. So, yeah, no literal obsessive washing, just... just a lot of emotional baggage that won't rinse off, no matter what I do.\n\nDoctor: Did these feelings start after a specific problem or change at work?\n\nPatient: Well, it's funny you ask that, because I've actually spent a lot of time trying to pinpoint exactly when this all started. That's when I decided to take on this sustainable plumbing certification course. \n\nI thought it was a great idea at the time, because, you know, everything is going green now, and I wanted to be ahead of the curve. My union hall was offering it, and I figured, 'Hey, I'm already in the apprenticeship, I might as well add this on and make myself more valuable.' But honestly, it's just been so much. It's not just the extra hours, which are a lot—I'm working my regular shifts, and then I have these evening classes and all this extra coursework about greywater systems and high-efficiency fixtures and all these new codes that are totally different from the standard stuff I'm just barely getting the hang of. \n\nSo, yeah, about five months ago, right when I started that certification on top of my regular apprenticeship duties, that's when this whole pattern really kicked in. I think the added pressure just sort of broke the dam. Suddenly I wasn't just worrying about getting my regular pipefitting right; I was worrying about all these new sustainable systems, and if I messed up a regular job, my brain would immediately jump to, 'Well, how are you ever going to handle the advanced green tech if you can't even sweat a basic copper joint perfectly?' It just snowballed from there, and for the last five months, it's just been this constant, heavy feeling that I'm failing at both things instead of succeeding at one.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry a lot about things outside of work too?\n\nPatient: Honestly, no, not really. It's funny, actually, because you'd think if someone was this stressed out, it would just bleed into everything, right? Like, I've got friends who worry about everything—the news, the economy, whether they offended the barista, just... everything. But for me, outside of work and this certification, my brain is actually pretty quiet. \n\nLike, take my family, for instance. I still live at home, and things are pretty chill there. My mom handles her stuff, my dad handles his, and I don't really sit around stressing about their health or if the house is going to fall apart or anything like that. And my social life, well, I don't really have a ton of time for one right now with the apprenticeship and the classes, but when I do hang out with my friends, I'm not obsessing over whether they secretly hate me or if I wore the wrong shirt. I just kind of exist. \n\nEven with money—like, apprentices don't make a ton, obviously, and gas prices are what they are, but I just budget it out and I don't lay awake at night staring at the ceiling, panicking about my bank account. It really is just this one specific lane. It's like my brain has this giant magnifying glass, and it's only focused on the pipefitting and the green tech course. If I'm not actively doing something related to the union or my classes, I'm actually pretty okay. I mean, I might be exhausted, but I'm not worried. It's just... as soon as I put on my steel-toe boots, or open that textbook on greywater systems, that's when the dread just drops like an anvil. But outside of that? No, I wouldn't say I worry about much else at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed much of the time?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean... no, not really depressed. I know I just talked a lot about that heavy, sinking feeling and the dread when I put on my boots, but it's not like a sadness, if that makes sense? It's not like I'm crying all the time or feeling hopeless about life in general. \n\nI actually had a buddy in high school who went through a really bad depression, and it was like he couldn't even get out of bed. He just stopped caring about everything—his car, his guitar, hanging out. He just kind of went blank. And I'm definitely not like that. I still get up every morning at 4:30 AM, make my coffee, pack my lunch—usually a turkey sandwich and an apple, nothing crazy—and I drive to the site or the shop. I still have energy to do the physical work, to lift the pipes and run the threader. I'm still engaged with the guys on the crew, joking around at break time, things like that. \n\nAnd when I get home, even though I'm tired from the physical labor and then having to study all that greywater system stuff, I don't feel... dark. It's the stress of the job and the certification that weighs on me, not a general feeling of being down. Like, if you took the apprenticeship and the classes away tomorrow, I think I'd feel completely light as a feather. I'd probably just sleep for two days straight, but I'd be perfectly happy. So, no, I wouldn't say I'm depressed. Just really, really stressed out about my performance and keeping up with everything I've taken on.\n\nDoctor: Has this stress started to interfere with how well you’re functioning at work or in class?\n\nPatient: Well, that's the thing, isn't it? That's kind of why I'm here, honestly. I mean, I try to keep it all together, I really do. I show up early, I bring my tools, I try to stay focused, but... yeah, it's definitely bleeding into the work now. We were roughing in the plumbing for this new commercial build, a mid-rise office building, and he had to point out that I was taking way, way too long measuring and cutting my cast iron. And he wasn't wrong. I was just standing there, double, triple, quadruple checking the measurements because I was so paralyzed by the thought of cutting it too short and wasting material. He told me I need to pick up the pace or I'm going to throw the whole crew behind schedule. \n\nAnd it's not just the site work, either. It's the classes. The certification course requires us to submit these detailed isometric sketches for different greywater routing scenarios, right? And I used to be pretty good at the drafting part, but lately, I just... I can't finish them. I sit down at my desk at home, I get my T-square and my pencils out, and I just stare at the paper. I'll draw a line, erase it, draw it again, and then I just get so overwhelmed that I put it away. My instructor has had to email me twice now, chasing me down for these assignments that are past due. \n\nSo, yeah, it's definitely interfering. I mean, I worked so hard to get into this apprenticeship. You have to pass an aptitude test, do a massive interview, wait on a list... it's a huge deal to get a spot. And then taking on the green tech certification was supposed to be this amazing career move. But now, with my foreman getting on my case about being slow, and my instructors hounding me for unfinished sketches... I'm just so scared I'm on the verge of totally wrecking both of them. I feel like I'm watching myself sabotage everything I've built, and I just can't seem to make my brain move fast enough or confidently enough to stop it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say this stress feels out of proportion to what most people in your situation would feel?\n\nPatient: Well... yeah, I guess so. I mean, I know it's a stressful program. Everyone says the first year of an apprenticeship is a grind, and adding the extra certification on top of it, I mean, everyone told me I was crazy to do it. But... I look around at the other first-years, like this guy Tommy on my crew. He's taking extra classes too, not the green tech one, but some advanced welding stuff at the community college. I just have to push through it somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, actually. And it’s—it's really weird, because I’ve always been someone who has to stay busy, you know? Like, outside of work and school, I usually have all these things going on. But lately... I don't know, it’s like someone just unplugged me. \n\nLike, take my sketchbook, for example. Not the drafting stuff for the certification, but my actual art sketchbook. I've always loved drawing, mostly charcoal and ink, just sketching people on the bus or doing these little architectural studies of old buildings downtown. I have this beautiful leather-bound book my mom got me when I got my associate's degree, and I used to take it literally everywhere. But lately, it's just sitting on my kitchen island, gathering dust. I can walk right past my sketchbook every single day, and I don't even feel a tiny urge to open it. \n\nAnd it’s not just that. I play on this intramural volleyball team on Thursday nights at the rec center—we're called the Spike Tysons, it's a terrible pun, I know, but we thought it was hilarious when we came up with it. I'm usually the setter, and I love those girls, we always go out for tacos after the games. But the last few weeks, I’ve just been skipping volleyball completely. I tell them I have to study for the green tech class, but really I’m just sitting in my apartment staring at the wall. And the weirdest part is, I don't even feel guilty about missing it. \n\nSame thing with my volunteer group. We do this community garden project on the weekends, which is usually right up my alley because I help them set up the drip irrigation systems for the raised vegetable beds. It's super rewarding, and I usually love figuring out the PVC layouts for them. But my phone will just be blowing up with the volunteer group texts, like, twenty messages back and forth about who is bringing the mulch this Saturday or what time we're starting, and I just... I completely ignore them. I swipe the notifications away without even reading them. \n\nAnd it's just so strange because lately I can walk past my sketchbook, skip volleyball, and ignore the volunteer group texts without feeling much of anything at all. No FOMO, no excitement, nothing. Even though those used to be the exact things that made me feel like myself, you know? Like, they were my outlet, the things that made me a whole person and not just a stressed-out apprentice who's always worried about cutting pipe wrong. Now it's just... flat. I just don't have the capacity to care about any of it right now, and I don't really understand why\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling this loss of interest nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I mean... I haven't exactly been marking it down on a calendar or anything, so I don't know if I can sit here and say it's literally every single day without fail. I'm really not sure. But... it definitely feels like it, if that makes sense? \n\nLike, the days just kind of blur together into this one long, flat line lately. I'll wake up—my alarm goes off at 4:30 AM because I have to beat the traffic to get to the job site before Dave starts his morning rant about punctuality—and I'll make my instant coffee, and I'll walk right past that leather sketchbook on the kitchen island. Monday, Tuesday, Wednesday... it's all exactly the same. I just look at it and feel absolutely nothing. \n\nAnd then I come home, and my shoulders are just aching from wrestling with pipe wrenches and cast iron all day, and normally that's when I'd want to do something to unwind, right? Like go to the community garden or at least text the Spike Tysons group chat to see what everyone's up to. But I just sit down on my couch. Sometimes I don't even take my steel-toe boots off for like an hour. I just sit there staring at the coffee table, knowing I'm tracking job-site dirt all over my rug, and I just don't have the desire to care enough to reach down and untie my laces. \n\nSo, I don't know if there was maybe one day in the last couple of weeks where I felt a tiny spark of wanting to do something—maybe there was,\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Sad? I don't... I don't think sad is the right word. It's not like I'm sitting around crying or feeling miserable all day. It's really more of that flat feeling I was talking about. Like, I'm just empty. \n\nI mean, there are definitely moments where I feel frustrated, like when Dave is yelling at me about the slope on a drain pipe and I know I measured it right, or when I'm staring at my green tech textbook and the words are just swimming around on the page because I'm so exhausted. In those moments, I might feel overwhelmed, maybe a little down about how hard everything is right now. But it's not a deep, persistent sadness, you know? \n\nIt's more like... I'm just existing. I get up, I go to work, I come home, I sit on the couch in my boots, and I go to sleep. Wash, rinse, repeat. I'm not walking around with this heavy cloud of sorrow hanging over my head. I'm just... I'm just not feeling much of anything at all. It's like the volume on my emotions has been turned all the way down to zero. So, no, I wouldn't say I feel sad most of the day. Just... blank. Which is almost worse, in a way, because at least if I were sad, I'd know I was still feeling something. But this? This is just... nothing.\n\nDoctor: Have you had trouble sleeping lately?\n\nPatient: Sleep? Oh man, sleep has been... it's been a whole thing, honestly. It's like my body doesn't even know what a normal sleep schedule is anymore. \n\nA few months ago, or maybe it was longer, I don't even know—but for a while there, I was barely sleeping at all. I'd be up until like 1:00 or 2:00 in the morning, just buzzing, you know? I'd be reorganizing my entire toolbox, color-coding my wrenches, or sketching out these massive, elaborate rainwater harvesting systems for my green tech class. And then my alarm would go off at 4:30 AM for work, and I'd just pop right up. Three, maybe four hours of sleep, tops. And the crazy thing is, I didn't even feel tired! I'd show up to the job site completely wired, practically vibrating with energy, ready to rough-in a whole commercial bathroom by myself before Dave even had his coffee. I felt like I had cracked the code to having more hours in the day. \n\nBut now? Now it's a completely different story. It's like the pendulum swung all the way to the other side, but in the worst way possible. I'll get into bed at a decent time, you know, because I'm just so physically exhausted from hauling pipe around all day and my shoulders are screaming, but I just... I can't fall asleep. I'll lie there staring at the ceiling fan, watching it spin, and my brain is just doing this weird, slow-motion crawl. Or, if I do finally manage to drift off, I'll wake up at like 3:00 AM, way before my alarm, and I just lie there in the dark. \n\nAnd when that 4:30 alarm finally does go off, I feel like I've been hit by a truck. It's like I never even closed my eyes. I drag myself out of bed, and my body feels like it's made of lead. It's just so frustrating because I'm so tired all the time now, but when I actually try to sleep, my body just refuses to cooperate. It's either I can't fall asleep in the first place, or I wake up way too early and just lie there feeling like I haven't rested for a single second. It makes getting through the day at the site so much harder, especially when I have to stay focused on safety stuff and not, you know, accidentally flood a basement because I'm too exhausted to double-check a fitting.\n\nDoctor: During that time when you were sleeping only 3 or 4 hours and still felt energized, did you also feel unusually high, euphoric, or more upbeat than normal?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... euphoric is a strong word, but I definitely felt... I don't know, untouchable? Like, there were these weeks—and this was back when I was still living in Miami before I moved up here for this apprenticeship—where I just felt almost too good. Like the whole city was mine. I'd be driving down I-95, windows down, blasting music, and I just felt this incredible, buzzing high, like I could do absolutely anything I wanted and nothing could stop me or even slow me down. \n\nI was taking on all these side jobs, fixing sinks and replacing toilets for cash on top of my regular hours, and I just felt so incredibly upbeat. Like, the sky was bluer, the air was warmer, everything just clicked. But the thing is, if anyone got in my way or tried to pump the brakes on me, I would just lose it. I remember this one time, this guy I was working with, Marco, he was taking forever to sweat this copper pipe, just moving at a snail's pace, and I completely snapped at him. I mean, way harder than I normally would. I basically yelled at him that if he couldn't keep up, he should just hand over the torch and go sit in the truck. Which, you know, wasn't cool, because Marco was a nice guy, but I just had zero patience. I was moving at a hundred miles an hour, and everyone else felt like they were stuck in molasses. So yeah, I was definitely more upbeat, but also just... really quick to get intensely irritated if people weren't on my exact wavelength.\n\nDoctor: During that time when you felt untouchable, were you talking more than usual?\n\nPatient: Talk? Oh my gosh, yes. I mean, it's actually kind of embarrassing to think about now, but at the time, I didn't even realize I was doing it. It was like my brain was moving so fast, and my mouth was just desperately trying to keep up with all the thoughts. \n\nI remember specifically in my green tech class, we were talking about, like, gray water recycling systems, and I had this whole grand idea about how we could retrofit older residential buildings. And I just started talking. And talking. And I couldn't stop. I was explaining the whole thing, from the initial pipe routing to the filtration setup, and I just kept going. I was talking over the instructor, talking over the other students. It was like I physically couldn't hit the brakes. I'd be barely taking a breath, just pushing the words out as fast as I could because if I stopped, I felt like I'd lose the thread of this incredibly important, brilliant idea I had.\n\nAnd it wasn't just in class, either. It was at the shop, too. I'd be trying to explain to Dave or Marco or whoever was around why we should organize the entire supply room differently, or how I figured out a faster way to sweat a joint. And I could see them, you know? I could see them looking at their watches, or shifting their weight, or literally picking up their tools to try and get back to work, but I just kept talking right at them. It was like I had this overwhelming urge to just keep speaking, and it didn't matter if they were listening or not. I just had to get it all out. It was exhausting for them, I'm sure, but for me, it just felt like my words were spilling out faster than I could control them."
